People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I came in because I’ve been having these really awful unwanted thoughts around a piece I was making for my aunt’s memorial. I’m an artist, so I’m used to second-guessing choices—like composition, colors, where a photo should go, that kind of thing—but this felt different.

It was like every tiny decision started carrying this horrible meaning. If I placed one photo slightly wrong, or picked the wrong color, my mind would jump to, “You’re dishonoring her,” or “This means you didn’t love her enough.” And I know, logically, that a photo placement doesn’t mean that, but I couldn’t just let the thought pass the way I normally can with creative doubts. It would stick and feel really upsetting, like I had to take it seriously even though I didn’t want to be thinking it at all.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
They felt like my own thoughts, not like a voice or something outside of me. That’s part of what made it so upsetting, honestly. It wasn’t like I thought someone else was putting them in my head—it was more like my own mind suddenly turned on this memorial piece and started attaching these terrible meanings to every choice I made.

Like I’d be looking at a photo of my aunt and thinking, “Should this go here, or would the balance be better over there?” which is normal for me as an artist, but then this other thought would just slam in: “If you put it there, you’re dishonoring her,” or, “If you choose the wrong color, it means you didn’t love her enough.” I knew that didn’t make sense in a rational way, but it still felt like it came from inside my own head, and I couldn’t brush it off like I usually can with ordinary creative doubts.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Mostly when I’m actually working on it, or even when I’m about to work on it and I’m thinking through the choices. Like if I’m sitting there with the photos laid out, or I’m looking at colors, that’s when it really grabs onto everything.

It’s not just a normal, “Oh, that composition isn’t quite right,” which I deal with all the time in my work. It becomes this awful thought that if I put one picture in the wrong spot, or choose the wrong shade, then somehow I’m dishonoring my aunt or it means I didn’t love her enough. And I know that sounds extreme, because logically I know a color choice doesn’t prove how I felt about her, but in the moment it feels so loaded that I can’t just move on from it.

So I’d say it’s tied to the memorial piece specifically—not just random thoughts out of nowhere all day—but it can start even before I’m physically working on it, if I’m thinking about what I still need to decide.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I mean, I’m still functioning, mostly. I don’t want to make it sound like everything has completely fallen apart, because I am still getting most of my work done, and I can still show up for things in general.

But it’s definitely costing me time in the studio. What should be a normal decision—like arranging something or choosing a color—can turn into this long, exhausting process where I’m stuck and rechecking myself or just sitting there trying to feel okay about it. And that extra time has started to have real consequences.

I missed a grant deadline because I lost so much time going back and forth on the memorial piece. That felt awful, because it wasn’t like I didn’t care about the grant—I did. I just couldn’t get myself unstuck enough to finish what I needed to submit. And I also backed out of a gallery talk that I had actually wanted to do, which is embarrassing to admit. I was already drained and behind, and I just felt like I couldn’t handle it on top of everything.

So relationships… I don’t know if it’s causing major conflict with people, exactly, but my daily work life has definitely been affected more than I want to admit.

They’ve kind of come and gone, but… not in a clean way, if that makes sense. It’s not like I had one thought once and then it disappeared. It kept coming back around the memorial piece, especially whenever I was making decisions about it.

Like, I’d be placing a photo or thinking about a color, and suddenly the thought would hit me that if I chose wrong, I was somehow dishonoring my aunt, or that it meant I didn’t love her enough. It felt repetitive and unwanted, not like normal artistic second-guessing. It wasn’t like I thought someone else was putting them in my head—it was more like my own mind suddenly turned on this memorial piece and started attaching these terrible meanings to every choice I made.\n\nLike I’d be looking at a photo of my aunt and thinking, “Should this go here, or would the balance be better over there?” which is normal for me as an artist, but then this other thought would just slam in: “If you put it there, you’re dishonoring her,” or, “If you choose the wrong color, it means you didn’t love her enough.” I knew that didn’t make sense in a rational way, but it still felt like it came from inside my own head, and I couldn’t brush it off like I usually can with ordinary creative doubts.\n\nDoctor: Have these unwanted thoughts about the memorial piece been happening only when you're working on it, or have they been popping up at other times as well?\n\nPatient: Mostly when I’m actually working on it, or even when I’m about to work on it and I’m thinking through the choices. Like if I’m sitting there with the photos laid out, or I’m looking at colors, that’s when it really grabs onto everything.\n\nIt’s not just a normal, “Oh, that composition isn’t quite right,” which I deal with all the time in my work. It becomes this awful thought that if I put one picture in the wrong spot, or choose the wrong shade, then somehow I’m dishonoring my aunt or it means I didn’t love her enough. And I know that sounds extreme, because logically I know a color choice doesn’t prove how I felt about her, but in the moment it feels so loaded that I can’t just move on from it.\n\nSo I’d say it’s tied to the memorial piece specifically—not just random thoughts out of nowhere all day—but it can start even before I’m physically working on it, if I’m thinking about what I still need to decide.\n\nDoctor: Have these thoughts been affecting your daily life or relationships in any significant way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’m still functioning, mostly. I don’t want to make it sound like everything has completely fallen apart, because I am still getting most of my work done, and I can still show up for things in general.\n\nBut it’s definitely costing me time in the studio. What should be a normal decision—like arranging something or choosing a color—can turn into this long, exhausting process where I’m stuck and rechecking myself or just sitting there trying to feel okay about it. And that extra time has started to have real consequences.\n\nI missed a grant deadline because I lost so much time going back and forth on the memorial piece. That felt awful, because it wasn’t like I didn’t care about the grant—I did. I just couldn’t get myself unstuck enough to finish what I needed to submit. And I also backed out of a gallery talk that I had actually wanted to do, which is embarrassing to admit. I was already drained and behind, and I just felt like I couldn’t handle it on top of everything.\n\nSo relationships… I don’t know if it’s causing major conflict with people, exactly, but my daily work life has definitely been affected more than I want to admit.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having these unwanted thoughts about the memorial piece continuously since they first started, or have they come and gone?\n\nPatient: They’ve kind of come and gone, but… not in a clean way, if that makes sense. It’s not like I had one thought once and then it disappeared. It kept coming back around the memorial piece, especially whenever I was making decisions about it.\n\nLike, I’d be placing a photo or thinking about a color, and suddenly the thought would hit me that if I chose wrong, I was somehow dishonoring my aunt, or that it meant I didn’t love her enough. And then sometimes I could step away and it would quiet down for a bit, but the next time I came back to the piece, there it was again. It felt repetitive and unwanted, not like normal artistic second-guessing. I mean, I second-guess my work all the time, but this had this horrible emotional weight to it that I couldn’t just brush off.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

It’s been pretty distressing, honestly. Not constant panic or anything, but when it hits, it feels awful and really disproportionate to what I’m actually doing.

I came in because when I’m working on my aunt’s memorial piece, I keep getting these thoughts that feel… almost cruel, like if I put one photo in the wrong place or pick the wrong color, then I’m somehow dishonoring her. Or that it means I didn’t love her enough, which is such a painful thing to have pop into my head when the whole reason I’m making the piece is because I loved her.

And I know, rationally, that a color choice or a layout decision doesn’t prove that. I can say that out loud and believe it on some level. But in the moment, it doesn’t feel like ordinary creative doubt. It feels like this heavy, guilty, urgent thing that I can’t just brush off. So I’d say the distress is definitely there—maybe not completely disabling, but enough that it really gets under my skin and makes working on it feel emotionally exhausting.

I’m not totally sure how to answer that, because I don’t think I’ve been avoiding it in a clear, deliberate way, like, “I refuse to work on this part.” It’s more slippery than that.

I do sometimes find myself kind of circling around the harder decisions, if that counts. Like I’ll tell myself I’m just gathering materials, or I’m just cleaning up the studio, or I’m just looking at references before I commit to something. And sometimes that’s normal for my process, honestly, so it’s hard for me to separate what’s avoidance from what’s just how I work.

With similar projects… I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed a pattern as clearly there. The memorial piece feels so specific and emotionally loaded that I’m not sure I can say it generalizes to everything else. I guess I’d say I’m not consciously avoiding a whole category of work, but I may be putting off certain decisions on this piece more than I’d like to admit.

Like if I’m sitting there with the photos laid out, or I’m looking at colors, that’s when it really grabs onto everything.\n\nIt’s not just a normal, “Oh, that composition isn’t quite right,” which I deal with all the time in my work. It becomes this awful thought that if I put one picture in the wrong spot, or choose the wrong shade, then somehow I’m dishonoring my aunt or it means I didn’t love her enough. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… sometimes. It’s definitely strongest when I’m actually working on the memorial piece, like when I’m choosing where a photo goes or trying to settle on a color, but it doesn’t stay neatly contained there.

I’ll be doing something else and it’ll sort of pop back in, like, “What if that choice was wrong?” or “What if I’m making something that doesn’t honor her the way it should?” And then it turns into this awful thought that maybe a wrong decision means I didn’t love her enough, which feels so unfair, because the whole reason I’m making it is because I did love her and I want it to mean something.

It’s not like I’m sitting there intentionally thinking through the artwork. It just intrudes, and then I feel pulled back into it emotionally. I can tell myself, “This is just a photo placement, this is just a color choice,” but it doesn’t always land that way in the moment. It feels much heavier than normal creative doubt.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I’ve been having these really awful unwanted thoughts around a piece I was making for my aunt’s memorial. I’m an artist, so I’m used to second-guessing choices—like composition, colors, where a photo should go, that kind of thing—but this felt different.\n\nIt was like every tiny decision started carrying this horrible meaning. If I placed one photo slightly wrong, or picked the wrong color, my mind would jump to, “You’re dishonoring her,” or “This means you didn’t love her enough.” And I know, logically, that a photo placement doesn’t mean that, but I couldn’t just let the thought pass the way I normally can with creative doubts. It would stick and feel really upsetting, like I had to take it seriously even though I didn’t want to be thinking it at all.\n\nDoctor: Did these thoughts feel like they were coming from outside of yourself or did they feel like your own thoughts?\n\nPatient: They felt like my own thoughts, not like a voice or something outside of me. That’s part of what made it so upsetting, honestly. It wasn’t like I thought someone else was putting them in my head—it was more like my own mind suddenly turned on this memorial piece and started attaching these terrible meanings to every choice I made.\n\nLike I’d be looking at a photo of my aunt and thinking, “Should this go here, or would the balance be better over there?” which is normal for me as an artist, but then this other thought would just slam in: “If you put it there, you’re dishonoring her,” or, “If you choose the wrong color, it means you didn’t love her enough.” I knew that didn’t make sense in a rational way, but it still felt like it came from inside my own head, and I couldn’t brush it off like I usually can with ordinary creative doubts.\n\nDoctor: Have these unwanted thoughts about the memorial piece been happening only when you're working on it, or have they been popping up at other times as well?\n\nPatient: Mostly when I’m actually working on it, or even when I’m about to work on it and I’m thinking through the choices. Like if I’m sitting there with the photos laid out, or I’m looking at colors, that’s when it really grabs onto everything.\n\nIt’s not just a normal, “Oh, that composition isn’t quite right,” which I deal with all the time in my work. It becomes this awful thought that if I put one picture in the wrong spot, or choose the wrong shade, then somehow I’m dishonoring my aunt or it means I didn’t love her enough. And I know that sounds extreme, because logically I know a color choice doesn’t prove how I felt about her, but in the moment it feels so loaded that I can’t just move on from it.\n\nSo I’d say it’s tied to the memorial piece specifically—not just random thoughts out of nowhere all day—but it can start even before I’m physically working on it, if I’m thinking about what I still need to decide.\n\nDoctor: Have these thoughts been affecting your daily life or relationships in any significant way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’m still functioning, mostly. I don’t want to make it sound like everything has completely fallen apart, because I am still getting most of my work done, and I can still show up for things in general.\n\nBut it’s definitely costing me time in the studio. What should be a normal decision—like arranging something or choosing a color—can turn into this long, exhausting process where I’m stuck and rechecking myself or just sitting there trying to feel okay about it. And that extra time has started to have real consequences.\n\nI missed a grant deadline because I lost so much time going back and forth on the memorial piece. That felt awful, because it wasn’t like I didn’t care about the grant—I did. I just couldn’t get myself unstuck enough to finish what I needed to submit. And I also backed out of a gallery talk that I had actually wanted to do, which is embarrassing to admit. I was already drained and behind, and I just felt like I couldn’t handle it on top of everything.\n\nSo relationships… I don’t know if it’s causing major conflict with people, exactly, but my daily work life has definitely been affected more than I want to admit.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having these unwanted thoughts about the memorial piece continuously since they first started, or have they come and gone?\n\nPatient: They’ve kind of come and gone, but… not in a clean way, if that makes sense. It’s not like I had one thought once and then it disappeared. It kept coming back around the memorial piece, especially whenever I was making decisions about it.\n\nLike, I’d be placing a photo or thinking about a color, and suddenly the thought would hit me that if I chose wrong, I was somehow dishonoring my aunt, or that it meant I didn’t love her enough. And then sometimes I could step away and it would quiet down for a bit, but the next time I came back to the piece, there it was again. It felt repetitive and unwanted, not like normal artistic second-guessing. I mean, I second-guess my work all the time, but this had this horrible emotional weight to it that I couldn’t just brush off.\n\nDoctor: How much distress would you say these unwanted thoughts have been causing you?\n\nPatient: It’s been pretty distressing, honestly. Not constant panic or anything, but when it hits, it feels awful and really disproportionate to what I’m actually doing.\n\nI came in because when I’m working on my aunt’s memorial piece, I keep getting these thoughts that feel… almost cruel, like if I put one photo in the wrong place or pick the wrong color, then I’m somehow dishonoring her. Or that it means I didn’t love her enough, which is such a painful thing to have pop into my head when the whole reason I’m making the piece is because I loved her.\n\nAnd I know, rationally, that a color choice or a layout decision doesn’t prove that. I can say that out loud and believe it on some level. But in the moment, it doesn’t feel like ordinary creative doubt. It feels like this heavy, guilty, urgent thing that I can’t just brush off. So I’d say the distress is definitely there—maybe not completely disabling, but enough that it really gets under my skin and makes working on it feel emotionally exhausting.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain aspects of the memorial piece or similar projects because of these unwanted thoughts?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, because I don’t think I’ve been avoiding it in a clear, deliberate way, like, “I refuse to work on this part.” It’s more slippery than that.\n\nI do sometimes find myself kind of circling around the harder decisions, if that counts. Like I’ll tell myself I’m just gathering materials, or I’m just cleaning up the studio, or I’m just looking at references before I commit to something. And sometimes that’s normal for my process, honestly, so it’s hard for me to separate what’s avoidance from what’s just how I work.\n\nWith similar projects… I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed a pattern as clearly there. The memorial piece feels so specific and emotionally loaded that I’m not sure I can say it generalizes to everything else. I guess I’d say I’m not consciously avoiding a whole category of work, but I may be putting off certain decisions on this piece more than I’d like to admit.\n\nDoctor: Do these unwanted thoughts about the memorial piece ever pop into your mind when you're not actively thinking about the piece or working on it?\n\nPatient: Yes… sometimes. It feels much heavier than normal creative doubt.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I don’t think it’s been damaging relationships in a big, obvious way, like I’m not fighting with people over it or anything. But it has started affecting what I can show up for, and I guess that indirectly affects people.

I’m still getting most of my actual work done, which is partly why I keep telling myself it’s not that serious. But the amount of extra time I’m losing in the studio—going back over decisions, hesitating, not moving forward as cleanly as I normally would—it’s started to have consequences. I missed a grant deadline because I just couldn’t get everything wrapped up in time, and that felt awful because it was something I really should have been able to submit for.

And I also backed out of a gallery talk I had actually wanted to do. I told myself I was just too overwhelmed and that it wasn’t the right time, but honestly, the studio delays and the emotional weight of the piece were a big part of it. So relationships, maybe not directly, but opportunities and commitments, yes. It’s starting to spill over more than I’d like to admit.

Like I’ll tell myself I’m just gathering materials, or I’m just cleaning up the studio, or I’m just looking at references before I commit to something. And sometimes that’s normal for my process, honestly, so it’s hard for me to separate what’s avoidance from what’s just how I work.\n\nWith similar projects… I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed a pattern as clearly there. The memorial piece feels so specific and emotionally loaded that I’m not sure I can say it generalizes to everything else. I guess I’d say I’m not consciously avoiding a whole category of work, but I may be putting off certain decisions on this piece more than I’d like to admit.\n\nDoctor: Do these unwanted thoughts about the memorial piece ever pop into your mind when you're not actively thinking about the piece or working on it?\n\nPatient: Yes… sometimes. It’s definitely strongest when I’m actually working on the memorial piece, like when I’m choosing where a photo goes or trying to settle on a color, but it doesn’t stay neatly contained there.\n\nI’ll be doing something else and it’ll sort of pop back in, like, “What if that choice was wrong?” or “What if I’m making something that doesn’t honor her the way it should?” And then it turns into this awful thought that maybe a wrong decision means I didn’t love her enough, which feels so unfair, because the whole reason I’m making it is because I did love her and I want it to mean something.\n\nIt’s not like I’m sitting there intentionally thinking through the artwork. It just intrudes, and then I feel pulled back into it emotionally. I can tell myself, “This is just a photo placement, this is just a color choice,” but it doesn’t always land that way in the moment. It feels much heavier than normal creative doubt.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts affecting any relationships outside of your work or daily activities?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s been damaging relationships in a big, obvious way, like I’m not fighting with people over it or anything. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
They feel very much in conflict with me. That’s part of why they scare me so much, I think.

I mean, I loved my aunt. I still love her. The whole reason I wanted to make the memorial piece was to honor her, not to reduce her to some perfect arrangement of photos or the exact right shade of blue or whatever. So when the thought comes in like, “If you put that image there, maybe you’re dishonoring her,” or, “If this color feels wrong, maybe it means you didn’t care enough,” it feels… almost offensive? Like it’s attacking the thing I care about most.

It doesn’t feel like my actual belief. I don’t honestly believe love works that way. If someone else were making something for someone they loved, I would never think one design choice proved anything about their love. I would probably tell them that the intention matters, that grief is messy, that a memorial doesn’t have to be perfect to be meaningful.

But when it’s me, with her, those thoughts land differently. They feel intrusive and unfair, and I can’t always just brush them off like normal creative doubt. Normal doubt is more like, “Is this composition working?” This feels like, “What if this choice means something terrible about you?” And that really doesn’t match what I believe, but it still gets under my skin.

It would stick and feel really upsetting, like I had to take it seriously even though I didn’t want to be thinking it at all.\n\nDoctor: Did these thoughts feel like they were coming from outside of yourself or did they feel like your own thoughts?\n\nPatient: They felt like my own thoughts, not like a voice or something outside of me. It wasn’t like I thought someone else was putting them in my head—it was more like my own mind suddenly turned on this memorial piece and started attaching these terrible meanings to every choice I made.\n\nLike I’d be looking at a photo of my aunt and thinking, “Should this go here, or would the balance be better over there?” which is normal for me as an artist, but then this other thought would just slam in: “If you put it there, you’re dishonoring her,” or, “If you choose the wrong color, it means you didn’t love her enough.” I knew that didn’t make sense in a rational way, but it still felt like it came from inside my own head, and I couldn’t brush it off like I usually can with ordinary creative doubts.\n\nDoctor: Have these unwanted thoughts about the memorial piece been happening only when you're working on it, or have they been popping up at other times as well?\n\nPatient: Mostly when I’m actually working on it, or even when I’m about to work on it and I’m thinking through the choices. Like if I’m sitting there with the photos laid out, or I’m looking at colors, that’s when it really grabs onto everything.\n\nIt’s not just a normal, “Oh, that composition isn’t quite right,” which I deal with all the time in my work. It becomes this awful thought that if I put one picture in the wrong spot, or choose the wrong shade, then somehow I’m dishonoring my aunt or it means I didn’t love her enough. And I know that sounds extreme, because logically I know a color choice doesn’t prove how I felt about her, but in the moment it feels so loaded that I can’t just move on from it.\n\nSo I’d say it’s tied to the memorial piece specifically—not just random thoughts out of nowhere all day—but it can start even before I’m physically working on it, if I’m thinking about what I still need to decide.\n\nDoctor: Have these thoughts been affecting your daily life or relationships in any significant way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’m still functioning, mostly. I don’t want to make it sound like everything has completely fallen apart, because I am still getting most of my work done, and I can still show up for things in general.\n\nBut it’s definitely costing me time in the studio. What should be a normal decision—like arranging something or choosing a color—can turn into this long, exhausting process where I’m stuck and rechecking myself or just sitting there trying to feel okay about it. And that extra time has started to have real consequences.\n\nI missed a grant deadline because I lost so much time going back and forth on the memorial piece. That felt awful, because it wasn’t like I didn’t care about the grant—I did. I just couldn’t get myself unstuck enough to finish what I needed to submit. And I also backed out of a gallery talk that I had actually wanted to do, which is embarrassing to admit. I was already drained and behind, and I just felt like I couldn’t handle it on top of everything.\n\nSo relationships… I don’t know if it’s causing major conflict with people, exactly, but my daily work life has definitely been affected more than I want to admit.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having these unwanted thoughts about the memorial piece continuously since they first started, or have they come and gone?\n\nPatient: They’ve kind of come and gone, but… not in a clean way, if that makes sense. It’s not like I had one thought once and then it disappeared. It kept coming back around the memorial piece, especially whenever I was making decisions about it.\n\nLike, I’d be placing a photo or thinking about a color, and suddenly the thought would hit me that if I chose wrong, I was somehow dishonoring my aunt, or that it meant I didn’t love her enough. And then sometimes I could step away and it would quiet down for a bit, but the next time I came back to the piece, there it was again. It felt repetitive and unwanted, not like normal artistic second-guessing. I mean, I second-guess my work all the time, but this had this horrible emotional weight to it that I couldn’t just brush off.\n\nDoctor: How much distress would you say these unwanted thoughts have been causing you?\n\nPatient: It’s been pretty distressing, honestly. Not constant panic or anything, but when it hits, it feels awful and really disproportionate to what I’m actually doing.\n\nI came in because when I’m working on my aunt’s memorial piece, I keep getting these thoughts that feel… almost cruel, like if I put one photo in the wrong place or pick the wrong color, then I’m somehow dishonoring her. Or that it means I didn’t love her enough, which is such a painful thing to have pop into my head when the whole reason I’m making the piece is because I loved her.\n\nAnd I know, rationally, that a color choice or a layout decision doesn’t prove that. I can say that out loud and believe it on some level. But in the moment, it doesn’t feel like ordinary creative doubt. It feels like this heavy, guilty, urgent thing that I can’t just brush off. So I’d say the distress is definitely there—maybe not completely disabling, but enough that it really gets under my skin and makes working on it feel emotionally exhausting.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain aspects of the memorial piece or similar projects because of these unwanted thoughts?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, because I don’t think I’ve been avoiding it in a clear, deliberate way, like, “I refuse to work on this part.” It’s more slippery than that.\n\nI do sometimes find myself kind of circling around the harder decisions, if that counts. Like I’ll tell myself I’m just gathering materials, or I’m just cleaning up the studio, or I’m just looking at references before I commit to something. And sometimes that’s normal for my process, honestly, so it’s hard for me to separate what’s avoidance from what’s just how I work.\n\nWith similar projects… I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed a pattern as clearly there. The memorial piece feels so specific and emotionally loaded that I’m not sure I can say it generalizes to everything else. I guess I’d say I’m not consciously avoiding a whole category of work, but I may be putting off certain decisions on this piece more than I’d like to admit.\n\nDoctor: Do these unwanted thoughts about the memorial piece ever pop into your mind when you're not actively thinking about the piece or working on it?\n\nPatient: Yes… sometimes. It’s definitely strongest when I’m actually working on the memorial piece, like when I’m choosing where a photo goes or trying to settle on a color, but it doesn’t stay neatly contained there.\n\nI’ll be doing something else and it’ll sort of pop back in, like, “What if that choice was wrong?” or “What if I’m making something that doesn’t honor her the way it should?” And then it turns into this awful thought that maybe a wrong decision means I didn’t love her enough, which feels so unfair, because the whole reason I’m making it is because I did love her and I want it to mean something.\n\nIt’s not like I’m sitting there intentionally thinking through the artwork. It just intrudes, and then I feel pulled back into it emotionally. I can tell myself, “This is just a photo placement, this is just a color choice,” but it doesn’t always land that way in the moment. It feels much heavier than normal creative doubt.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts affecting any relationships outside of your work or daily activities?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s been damaging relationships in a big, obvious way, like I’m not fighting with people over it or anything. But it has started affecting what I can show up for, and I guess that indirectly affects people.\n\nI’m still getting most of my actual work done, which is partly why I keep telling myself it’s not that serious. But the amount of extra time I’m losing in the studio—going back over decisions, hesitating, not moving forward as cleanly as I normally would—it’s started to have consequences. I missed a grant deadline because I just couldn’t get everything wrapped up in time, and that felt awful because it was something I really should have been able to submit for.\n\nAnd I also backed out of a gallery talk I had actually wanted to do. I told myself I was just too overwhelmed and that it wasn’t the right time, but honestly, the studio delays and the emotional weight of the piece were a big part of it. So relationships, maybe not directly, but opportunities and commitments, yes. It’s starting to spill over more than I’d like to admit.\n\nDoctor: When these unwanted thoughts come up, do you feel like they align with your own values and beliefs, or do they feel like they're in conflict with them?\n\nPatient: They feel very much in conflict with me. That’s part of why they scare me so much, I think.\n\nI mean, I loved my aunt. I still love her. The whole reason I wanted to make the memorial piece was to honor her, not to reduce her to some perfect arrangement of photos or the exact right shade of blue or whatever. So when the thought comes in like, “If you put that image there, maybe you’re dishonoring her,” or, “If this color feels wrong, maybe it means you didn’t care enough,” it feels… almost offensive? Like it’s attacking the thing I care about most.\n\nIt doesn’t feel like my actual belief. I don’t honestly believe love works that way. If someone else were making something for someone they loved, I would never think one design choice proved anything about their love. I would probably tell them that the intention matters, that grief is messy, that a memorial doesn’t have to be perfect to be meaningful.\n\nBut when it’s me, with her, those thoughts land differently. They feel intrusive and unfair, and I can’t always just brush them off like normal creative doubt. Normal doubt is more like, “Is this composition working?” This feels like, “What if this choice means something terrible about you?” And that really doesn’t match what I believe, but it still gets under my skin.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

But it has started affecting what I can show up for, and I guess that indirectly affects people.\n\nI’m still getting most of my actual work done, which is partly why I keep telling myself it’s not that serious. But the amount of extra time I’m losing in the studio—going back over decisions, hesitating, not moving forward as cleanly as I normally would—it’s started to have consequences. I missed a grant deadline because I just couldn’t get everything wrapped up in time, and that felt awful because it was something I really should have been able to submit for.\n\nAnd I also backed out of a gallery talk I had actually wanted to do. I told myself I was just too overwhelmed and that it wasn’t the right time, but honestly, the studio delays and the emotional weight of the piece were a big part of it. So relationships, maybe not directly, but opportunities and commitments, yes. It’s starting to spill over more than I’d like to admit.\n\nDoctor: When these unwanted thoughts come up, do you feel like they align with your own values and beliefs, or do they feel like they're in conflict with them?\n\nPatient: They feel very much in conflict with me. That’s part of why they scare me so much, I think.\n\nI mean, I loved my aunt. I still love her. The whole reason I wanted to make the memorial piece was to honor her, not to reduce her to some perfect arrangement of photos or the exact right shade of blue or whatever. So when the thought comes in like, “If you put that image there, maybe you’re dishonoring her,” or, “If this color feels wrong, maybe it means you didn’t care enough,” it feels… almost offensive? Like it’s attacking the thing I care about most.\n\nIt doesn’t feel like my actual belief. I don’t honestly believe love works that way. If someone else were making something for someone they loved, I would never think one design choice proved anything about their love. I would probably tell them that the intention matters, that grief is messy, that a memorial doesn’t have to be perfect to be meaningful.\n\nBut when it’s me, with her, those thoughts land differently. They feel intrusive and unfair, and I can’t always just brush them off like normal creative doubt. Normal doubt is more like, “Is this composition working?” This feels like, “What if this choice means something terrible about you?” And that really doesn’t match what I believe, but it still gets under my skin.\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen these thoughts come to you, do you feel like they are a part of yourself or do they feel foreign and not aligned with who you are?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 32 / artist_or_related_worker / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Obsessions\nmanifestation: \"I came in because when I was working on my aunt’s memorial piece, I kept getting these awful, unwanted thoughts that I was going to dishonor her somehow—like one wrong photo placement or color choice would mean I didn’t love her enough—and I couldn’t just brush them off like normal creative doubts.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
They feel foreign. I mean, they happen in my mind, obviously, so in that sense they’re “mine,” but they don’t feel like they come from the part of me that actually knows my aunt or loves her.

It’s more like this harsh, unwanted voice cutting in while I’m trying to make something meaningful. I’ll be looking at a photo placement or a color choice, something that should just be part of the creative process, and suddenly it becomes, “What if this dishonors her?” or, “What if getting this wrong means you didn’t love her enough?” And that feels completely out of line with who I am.

I don’t believe love is measured by whether a memorial piece is perfectly arranged. I came in because I couldn’t just brush those thoughts off like normal creative doubts. They felt intrusive, like they were attacking the whole purpose of the piece, not expressing what I really think or feel.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.2, F48.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I’ve been having these really awful unwanted thoughts around a piece I was making for my aunt’s memorial. I’m an artist, so I’m used to second-guessing choices—like composition, colors, where a photo should go, that kind of thing—but this felt different.\n\nIt was like every tiny decision started carrying this horrible meaning. If I placed one photo slightly wrong, or picked the wrong color, my mind would jump to, “You’re dishonoring her,” or “This means you didn’t love her enough.” And I know, logically, that a photo placement doesn’t mean that, but I couldn’t just let the thought pass the way I normally can with creative doubts. It would stick and feel really upsetting, like I had to take it seriously even though I didn’t want to be thinking it at all.\n\nDoctor: Did these thoughts feel like they were coming from outside of yourself or did they feel like your own thoughts?\n\nPatient: They felt like my own thoughts, not like a voice or something outside of me. That’s part of what made it so upsetting, honestly. It wasn’t like I thought someone else was putting them in my head—it was more like my own mind suddenly turned on this memorial piece and started attaching these terrible meanings to every choice I made.\n\nLike I’d be looking at a photo of my aunt and thinking, “Should this go here, or would the balance be better over there?” which is normal for me as an artist, but then this other thought would just slam in: “If you put it there, you’re dishonoring her,” or, “If you choose the wrong color, it means you didn’t love her enough.” I knew that didn’t make sense in a rational way, but it still felt like it came from inside my own head, and I couldn’t brush it off like I usually can with ordinary creative doubts.\n\nDoctor: Have these unwanted thoughts about the memorial piece been happening only when you're working on it, or have they been popping up at other times as well?\n\nPatient: Mostly when I’m actually working on it, or even when I’m about to work on it and I’m thinking through the choices. Like if I’m sitting there with the photos laid out, or I’m looking at colors, that’s when it really grabs onto everything.\n\nIt’s not just a normal, “Oh, that composition isn’t quite right,” which I deal with all the time in my work. It becomes this awful thought that if I put one picture in the wrong spot, or choose the wrong shade, then somehow I’m dishonoring my aunt or it means I didn’t love her enough. And I know that sounds extreme, because logically I know a color choice doesn’t prove how I felt about her, but in the moment it feels so loaded that I can’t just move on from it.\n\nSo I’d say it’s tied to the memorial piece specifically—not just random thoughts out of nowhere all day—but it can start even before I’m physically working on it, if I’m thinking about what I still need to decide.\n\nDoctor: Have these thoughts been affecting your daily life or relationships in any significant way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’m still functioning, mostly. I don’t want to make it sound like everything has completely fallen apart, because I am still getting most of my work done, and I can still show up for things in general.\n\nBut it’s definitely costing me time in the studio. What should be a normal decision—like arranging something or choosing a color—can turn into this long, exhausting process where I’m stuck and rechecking myself or just sitting there trying to feel okay about it. And that extra time has started to have real consequences.\n\nI missed a grant deadline because I lost so much time going back and forth on the memorial piece. That felt awful, because it wasn’t like I didn’t care about the grant—I did. I just couldn’t get myself unstuck enough to finish what I needed to submit. And I also backed out of a gallery talk that I had actually wanted to do, which is embarrassing to admit. I was already drained and behind, and I just felt like I couldn’t handle it on top of everything.\n\nSo relationships… I don’t know if it’s causing major conflict with people, exactly, but my daily work life has definitely been affected more than I want to admit.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having these unwanted thoughts about the memorial piece continuously since they first started, or have they come and gone?\n\nPatient: They’ve kind of come and gone, but… not in a clean way, if that makes sense. It’s not like I had one thought once and then it disappeared. It kept coming back around the memorial piece, especially whenever I was making decisions about it.\n\nLike, I’d be placing a photo or thinking about a color, and suddenly the thought would hit me that if I chose wrong, I was somehow dishonoring my aunt, or that it meant I didn’t love her enough. And then sometimes I could step away and it would quiet down for a bit, but the next time I came back to the piece, there it was again. It felt repetitive and unwanted, not like normal artistic second-guessing. I mean, I second-guess my work all the time, but this had this horrible emotional weight to it that I couldn’t just brush off.\n\nDoctor: How much distress would you say these unwanted thoughts have been causing you?\n\nPatient: It’s been pretty distressing, honestly. Not constant panic or anything, but when it hits, it feels awful and really disproportionate to what I’m actually doing.\n\nI came in because when I’m working on my aunt’s memorial piece, I keep getting these thoughts that feel… almost cruel, like if I put one photo in the wrong place or pick the wrong color, then I’m somehow dishonoring her. Or that it means I didn’t love her enough, which is such a painful thing to have pop into my head when the whole reason I’m making the piece is because I loved her.\n\nAnd I know, rationally, that a color choice or a layout decision doesn’t prove that. I can say that out loud and believe it on some level. But in the moment, it doesn’t feel like ordinary creative doubt. It feels like this heavy, guilty, urgent thing that I can’t just brush off. So I’d say the distress is definitely there—maybe not completely disabling, but enough that it really gets under my skin and makes working on it feel emotionally exhausting.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain aspects of the memorial piece or similar projects because of these unwanted thoughts?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, because I don’t think I’ve been avoiding it in a clear, deliberate way, like, “I refuse to work on this part.” It’s more slippery than that.\n\nI do sometimes find myself kind of circling around the harder decisions, if that counts. Like I’ll tell myself I’m just gathering materials, or I’m just cleaning up the studio, or I’m just looking at references before I commit to something. And sometimes that’s normal for my process, honestly, so it’s hard for me to separate what’s avoidance from what’s just how I work.\n\nWith similar projects… I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed a pattern as clearly there. The memorial piece feels so specific and emotionally loaded that I’m not sure I can say it generalizes to everything else. I guess I’d say I’m not consciously avoiding a whole category of work, but I may be putting off certain decisions on this piece more than I’d like to admit.\n\nDoctor: Do these unwanted thoughts about the memorial piece ever pop into your mind when you're not actively thinking about the piece or working on it?\n\nPatient: Yes… sometimes. It’s definitely strongest when I’m actually working on the memorial piece, like when I’m choosing where a photo goes or trying to settle on a color, but it doesn’t stay neatly contained there.\n\nI’ll be doing something else and it’ll sort of pop back in, like, “What if that choice was wrong?” or “What if I’m making something that doesn’t honor her the way it should?” And then it turns into this awful thought that maybe a wrong decision means I didn’t love her enough, which feels so unfair, because the whole reason I’m making it is because I did love her and I want it to mean something.\n\nIt’s not like I’m sitting there intentionally thinking through the artwork. It just intrudes, and then I feel pulled back into it emotionally. I can tell myself, “This is just a photo placement, this is just a color choice,” but it doesn’t always land that way in the moment. It feels much heavier than normal creative doubt.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts affecting any relationships outside of your work or daily activities?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s been damaging relationships in a big, obvious way, like I’m not fighting with people over it or anything. But it has started affecting what I can show up for, and I guess that indirectly affects people.\n\nI’m still getting most of my actual work done, which is partly why I keep telling myself it’s not that serious. But the amount of extra time I’m losing in the studio—going back over decisions, hesitating, not moving forward as cleanly as I normally would—it’s started to have consequences. I missed a grant deadline because I just couldn’t get everything wrapped up in time, and that felt awful because it was something I really should have been able to submit for.\n\nAnd I also backed out of a gallery talk I had actually wanted to do. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not altogether, no. I’m not completely avoiding it in the sense of refusing to go into the studio or abandoning the piece. I’m still working, and honestly I’m still getting most of my work done overall.

But I do think I avoid parts of it, or I slow down so much that it almost functions like avoidance. Like I’ll sit there with the memorial piece and spend way too long rechecking a choice or circling around a decision instead of actually moving forward. And then the day gets eaten up. So from the outside it might look like I’m working, but I’m losing a lot of time.

And it has had real consequences already. I missed a grant deadline because I couldn’t get everything wrapped up in time, which was really upsetting because it was something I should have been able to submit. And I backed out of a gallery talk I had wanted to do. I told myself I was just overwhelmed, but the delays in the studio were a big part of it.

So I wouldn’t say I’m avoiding all of it completely, but it’s definitely interfering more than I want to admit.

It becomes this awful thought that if I put one picture in the wrong spot, or choose the wrong shade, then somehow I’m dishonoring my aunt or it means I didn’t love her enough. It’s not like I had one thought once and then it disappeared. It kept coming back around the memorial piece, especially whenever I was making decisions about it.\n\nLike, I’d be placing a photo or thinking about a color, and suddenly the thought would hit me that if I chose wrong, I was somehow dishonoring my aunt, or that it meant I didn’t love her enough. Not constant panic or anything, but when it hits, it feels awful and really disproportionate to what I’m actually doing.\n\nI came in because when I’m working on my aunt’s memorial piece, I keep getting these thoughts that feel… almost cruel, like if I put one photo in the wrong place or pick the wrong color, then I’m somehow dishonoring her. It wasn’t like I thought someone else was putting them in my head—it was more like my own mind suddenly turned on this memorial piece and started attaching these terrible meanings to every choice I made.\n\nLike I’d be looking at a photo of my aunt and thinking, “Should this go here, or would the balance be better over there?” which is normal for me as an artist, but then this other thought would just slam in: “If you put it there, you’re dishonoring her,” or, “If you choose the wrong color, it means you didn’t love her enough.” I knew that didn’t make sense in a rational way, but it still felt like it came from inside my own head, and I couldn’t brush it off like I usually can with ordinary creative doubts.\n\nDoctor: Have these unwanted thoughts about the memorial piece been happening only when you're working on it, or have they been popping up at other times as well?\n\nPatient: Mostly when I’m actually working on it, or even when I’m about to work on it and I’m thinking through the choices. Like if I’m sitting there with the photos laid out, or I’m looking at colors, that’s when it really grabs onto everything.\n\nIt’s not just a normal, “Oh, that composition isn’t quite right,” which I deal with all the time in my work. It becomes this awful thought that if I put one picture in the wrong spot, or choose the wrong shade, then somehow I’m dishonoring my aunt or it means I didn’t love her enough. And I know that sounds extreme, because logically I know a color choice doesn’t prove how I felt about her, but in the moment it feels so loaded that I can’t just move on from it.\n\nSo I’d say it’s tied to the memorial piece specifically—not just random thoughts out of nowhere all day—but it can start even before I’m physically working on it, if I’m thinking about what I still need to decide.\n\nDoctor: Have these thoughts been affecting your daily life or relationships in any significant way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’m still functioning, mostly. I don’t want to make it sound like everything has completely fallen apart, because I am still getting most of my work done, and I can still show up for things in general.\n\nBut it’s definitely costing me time in the studio. What should be a normal decision—like arranging something or choosing a color—can turn into this long, exhausting process where I’m stuck and rechecking myself or just sitting there trying to feel okay about it. And that extra time has started to have real consequences.\n\nI missed a grant deadline because I lost so much time going back and forth on the memorial piece. That felt awful, because it wasn’t like I didn’t care about the grant—I did. I just couldn’t get myself unstuck enough to finish what I needed to submit. And I also backed out of a gallery talk that I had actually wanted to do, which is embarrassing to admit. I was already drained and behind, and I just felt like I couldn’t handle it on top of everything.\n\nSo relationships… I don’t know if it’s causing major conflict with people, exactly, but my daily work life has definitely been affected more than I want to admit.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having these unwanted thoughts about the memorial piece continuously since they first started, or have they come and gone?\n\nPatient: They’ve kind of come and gone, but… not in a clean way, if that makes sense. It’s not like I had one thought once and then it disappeared. It kept coming back around the memorial piece, especially whenever I was making decisions about it.\n\nLike, I’d be placing a photo or thinking about a color, and suddenly the thought would hit me that if I chose wrong, I was somehow dishonoring my aunt, or that it meant I didn’t love her enough. And then sometimes I could step away and it would quiet down for a bit, but the next time I came back to the piece, there it was again. It felt repetitive and unwanted, not like normal artistic second-guessing. I mean, I second-guess my work all the time, but this had this horrible emotional weight to it that I couldn’t just brush off.\n\nDoctor: How much distress would you say these unwanted thoughts have been causing you?\n\nPatient: It’s been pretty distressing, honestly. Not constant panic or anything, but when it hits, it feels awful and really disproportionate to what I’m actually doing.\n\nI came in because when I’m working on my aunt’s memorial piece, I keep getting these thoughts that feel… almost cruel, like if I put one photo in the wrong place or pick the wrong color, then I’m somehow dishonoring her. Or that it means I didn’t love her enough, which is such a painful thing to have pop into my head when the whole reason I’m making the piece is because I loved her.\n\nAnd I know, rationally, that a color choice or a layout decision doesn’t prove that. I can say that out loud and believe it on some level. But in the moment, it doesn’t feel like ordinary creative doubt. It feels like this heavy, guilty, urgent thing that I can’t just brush off. So I’d say the distress is definitely there—maybe not completely disabling, but enough that it really gets under my skin and makes working on it feel emotionally exhausting.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain aspects of the memorial piece or similar projects because of these unwanted thoughts?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, because I don’t think I’ve been avoiding it in a clear, deliberate way, like, “I refuse to work on this part.” It’s more slippery than that.\n\nI do sometimes find myself kind of circling around the harder decisions, if that counts. Like I’ll tell myself I’m just gathering materials, or I’m just cleaning up the studio, or I’m just looking at references before I commit to something. And sometimes that’s normal for my process, honestly, so it’s hard for me to separate what’s avoidance from what’s just how I work.\n\nWith similar projects… I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed a pattern as clearly there. The memorial piece feels so specific and emotionally loaded that I’m not sure I can say it generalizes to everything else. I guess I’d say I’m not consciously avoiding a whole category of work, but I may be putting off certain decisions on this piece more than I’d like to admit.\n\nDoctor: Do these unwanted thoughts about the memorial piece ever pop into your mind when you're not actively thinking about the piece or working on it?\n\nPatient: Yes… sometimes. It’s definitely strongest when I’m actually working on the memorial piece, like when I’m choosing where a photo goes or trying to settle on a color, but it doesn’t stay neatly contained there.\n\nI’ll be doing something else and it’ll sort of pop back in, like, “What if that choice was wrong?” or “What if I’m making something that doesn’t honor her the way it should?” And then it turns into this awful thought that maybe a wrong decision means I didn’t love her enough, which feels so unfair, because the whole reason I’m making it is because I did love her and I want it to mean something.\n\nIt’s not like I’m sitting there intentionally thinking through the artwork. It just intrudes, and then I feel pulled back into it emotionally. I can tell myself, “This is just a photo placement, this is just a color choice,” but it doesn’t always land that way in the moment. It feels much heavier than normal creative doubt.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts affecting any relationships outside of your work or daily activities?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s been damaging relationships in a big, obvious way, like I’m not fighting with people over it or anything. But it has started affecting what I can show up for, and I guess that indirectly affects people.\n\nI’m still getting most of my actual work done, which is partly why I keep telling myself it’s not that serious. But the amount of extra time I’m losing in the studio—going back over decisions, hesitating, not moving forward as cleanly as I normally would—it’s started to have consequences. I missed a grant deadline because I just couldn’t get everything wrapped up in time, and that felt awful because it was something I really should have been able to submit for.\n\nAnd I also backed out of a gallery talk I had actually wanted to do. I told myself I was just too overwhelmed and that it wasn’t the right time, but honestly, the studio delays and the emotional weight of the piece were a big part of it. So relationships, maybe not directly, but opportunities and commitments, yes. It’s starting to spill over more than I’d like to admit.\n\nDoctor: When these unwanted thoughts come up, do you feel like they align with your own values and beliefs, or do they feel like they're in conflict with them?\n\nPatient: They feel very much in conflict with me. That’s part of why they scare me so much, I think.\n\nI mean, I loved my aunt. I still love her. The whole reason I wanted to make the memorial piece was to honor her, not to reduce her to some perfect arrangement of photos or the exact right shade of blue or whatever. So when the thought comes in like, “If you put that image there, maybe you’re dishonoring her,” or, “If this color feels wrong, maybe it means you didn’t care enough,” it feels… almost offensive? Like it’s attacking the thing I care about most.\n\nIt doesn’t feel like my actual belief. I don’t honestly believe love works that way. If someone else were making something for someone they loved, I would never think one design choice proved anything about their love. I would probably tell them that the intention matters, that grief is messy, that a memorial doesn’t have to be perfect to be meaningful.\n\nBut when it’s me, with her, those thoughts land differently. They feel intrusive and unfair, and I can’t always just brush them off like normal creative doubt. Normal doubt is more like, “Is this composition working?” This feels like, “What if this choice means something terrible about you?” And that really doesn’t match what I believe, but it still gets under my skin.\n\nDoctor: When these thoughts come to you, do you feel like they are a part of yourself or do they feel foreign and not aligned with who you are?\n\nPatient: They feel foreign. I mean, they happen in my mind, obviously, so in that sense they’re “mine,” but they don’t feel like they come from the part of me that actually knows my aunt or loves her.\n\nIt’s more like this harsh, unwanted voice cutting in while I’m trying to make something meaningful. I’ll be looking at a photo placement or a color choice, something that should just be part of the creative process, and suddenly it becomes, “What if this dishonors her?” or, “What if getting this wrong means you didn’t love her enough?” And that feels completely out of line with who I am.\n\nI don’t believe love is measured by whether a memorial piece is perfectly arranged. I came in because I couldn’t just brush those thoughts off like normal creative doubts. They felt intrusive, like they were attacking the whole purpose of the piece, not expressing what I really think or feel.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding working on the memorial piece or similar projects altogether because of these unwanted thoughts?\n\nPatient: Not altogether, no. I’m not completely avoiding it in the sense of refusing to go into the studio or abandoning the piece. I’m still working, and honestly I’m still getting most of my work done overall.\n\nBut I do think I avoid parts of it, or I slow down so much that it almost functions like avoidance. Like I’ll sit there with the memorial piece and spend way too long rechecking a choice or circling around a decision instead of actually moving forward. And then the day gets eaten up. So from the outside it might look like I’m working, but I’m losing a lot of time.\n\nAnd it has had real consequences already. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. I do, and that’s the part that makes it feel less like normal worrying and more like I’m getting pulled into something.

When the thought hits, I’ll feel this pressure to fix it somehow, like I can’t just leave the layout alone. I’ll reread the label text again and again, checking if the wording sounds respectful enough or if some phrase could be taken the wrong way. And then I’ll nudge the same objects on the table, just a tiny bit, like moving a photo half an inch or adjusting the spacing, and I’ll tell myself I’m just refining it, but really I’m trying to get this uncomfortable feeling to stop.

I also keep checking the layout photos over and over. I’ll take a picture, look at it, zoom in, compare it to the previous version, then maybe move something back to where it already was. It’s embarrassing because part of me knows it’s already fine. Like, if I stepped back, I could probably say, “This is good enough, this is thoughtful, this is done.” But it doesn’t feel settled, and I keep chasing that feeling of, okay, now it’s safe, now it’s right, now nothing bad is implied by it.

And I don’t literally think moving one object will prevent something terrible in a logical way, but emotionally it feels like if I don’t check or adjust it, I’m leaving open the possibility that I’ve done something wrong. So I lose studio time trying to make the feeling go away.
