I missed a grant deadline because I couldn’t get everything finished and submitted in time, which felt awful because I really should have been able to do it. And I backed out of a gallery talk that I had actually wanted to do. It wasn’t that I didn’t care about it — I did — but I felt so behind and tangled up in the studio that I couldn’t face it.\n\nSo, yes, it’s causing distress. Maybe not ruining every part of my life, but it’s definitely affecting my work and making things I normally care about harder to enjoy.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the memorial piece needs to be perfectly symmetrical or exact in some way to feel right to you?\n\nPatient: Not exactly symmetrical, no. It’s not like I need everything measured with a ruler or perfectly mirrored. It’s more that it has to feel… settled, I guess. Like the arrangement has to land in a way where I don’t get that jolt of, “Wait, is this wrong?”\n\nSo I’ll end up rereading the label text again, and then I’ll look at the objects on the table and nudge the same ones over and over — just tiny adjustments, sometimes so small they probably don’t even matter. Then I’ll check the layout photos, compare them, zoom in, go back to the table, move something, take another photo, and check again.\n\nAnd the frustrating thing is that part of me knows it’s already fine. I can see that it’s thoughtful and that nothing is obviously wrong with it. But it doesn’t feel done until I get this internal sense of, okay, that’s settled. And I keep chasing that feeling, which means I lose a lot of studio time to it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you need to have complete control over every aspect of the memorial piece to feel comfortable with it?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say complete control over every aspect, exactly. That sounds almost too broad, like I’m trying to control the whole meaning of the piece or how every person will react to it, and I know I can’t really do that.\n\nBut with the actual layout, yes, I get pulled into needing it to feel controlled enough that I can relax. Like I’ll reread the label text again, even if I already read it five minutes ago, because I’m checking whether it lands the right way. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… that’s pretty much it, though I know “perfect” sounds dramatic. It’s not like I honestly believe there is one flawless version of the piece and anything else is worthless. But emotionally, when I’m in it, it starts to feel that way.

I keep having these thoughts that if I choose the wrong photo, or place something slightly wrong, or use a color that doesn’t feel right, then somehow I’ve dishonored her. Like it would mean I didn’t understand her, or didn’t love her enough, or wasn’t careful enough with her memory. And I know, saying it out loud, that one photo placement doesn’t actually prove how much I loved my aunt. I do know that. But the thought hits me as if it’s true, and it’s really hard to just let it pass.

That’s actually why I came in. It stopped feeling like normal creative uncertainty. I’m used to having doubts in the studio — that’s part of making work — but this feels more awful and sticky. It’s unwanted, and it comes with this feeling that I could do something wrong that would be disrespectful to her, even if no one else would see it that way. So, yes, in that sense, it feels like it has to be exactly right before I can feel like I’ve done right by her.

But then that feeling doesn’t last, or it doesn’t come at all, and I lose studio time going around in circles.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the memorial piece has to be perfect in order for you to feel like you've done your aunt justice?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s pretty much it, though I know “perfect” sounds dramatic. It’s not like I honestly believe there is one flawless version of the piece and anything else is worthless. But emotionally, when I’m in it, it starts to feel that way.\n\nI keep having these thoughts that if I choose the wrong photo, or place something slightly wrong, or use a color that doesn’t feel right, then somehow I’ve dishonored her. Like it would mean I didn’t understand her, or didn’t love her enough, or wasn’t careful enough with her memory. And I know, saying it out loud, that one photo placement doesn’t actually prove how much I loved my aunt. I do know that. But the thought hits me as if it’s true, and it’s really hard to just let it pass.\n\nThat’s actually why I came in. It stopped feeling like normal creative uncertainty. I’m used to having doubts in the studio — that’s part of making work — but this feels more awful and sticky. It’s unwanted, and it comes with this feeling that I could do something wrong that would be disrespectful to her, even if no one else would see it that way. So, yes, in that sense, it feels like it has to be exactly right before I can feel like I’ve done right by her.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I do, and that’s the part that makes it feel less like normal worrying and more like I’m getting pulled into something.\n\nWhen the thought hits, I’ll feel this pressure to fix it somehow, like I can’t just leave the layout alone. I’ll reread the label text again and again, checking if the wording sounds respectful enough or if some phrase could be taken the wrong way. And then I’ll nudge the same objects on the table, just a tiny bit, like moving a photo half an inch or adjusting the spacing, and I’ll tell myself I’m just refining it, but really I’m trying to get this uncomfortable feeling to stop.\n\nI also keep checking the layout photos over and over. I’ll take a picture, look at it, zoom in, compare it to the previous version, then maybe move something back to where it already was. It’s embarrassing because part of me knows it’s already fine. Like, if I stepped back, I could probably say, “This is good enough, this is thoughtful, this is done.” But it doesn’t feel settled, and I keep chasing that feeling of, okay, now it’s safe, now it’s right, now nothing bad is implied by it.\n\nAnd I don’t literally think moving one object will prevent something terrible in a logical way, but emotionally it feels like if I don’t check or adjust it, I’m leaving open the possibility that I’ve done something wrong. It’s not like I need everything measured with a ruler or perfectly mirrored. It’s more that it has to feel… settled, I guess. Like the arrangement has to land in a way where I don’t get that jolt of, “Wait, is this wrong?”\n\nSo I’ll end up rereading the label text again, and then I’ll look at the objects on the table and nudge the same ones over and over — just tiny adjustments, sometimes so small they probably don’t even matter. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… that’s the fear, I think. “Tarnished” maybe sounds a little intense, but that’s kind of how it feels in the moment. Like if the piece isn’t right — if I choose the wrong photo, or the color feels off, or something is placed in a way that doesn’t feel careful enough — then it’s not just an art decision anymore. It starts to feel like I’ve done something disrespectful to her memory.

And I know that doesn’t fully make sense. I mean, if someone else said that to me, I’d probably tell them, of course one photo placement doesn’t mean you didn’t love your aunt. But when I was working on it, I kept getting these awful, unwanted thoughts that I was going to dishonor her somehow. Like the piece would reveal that I didn’t understand her well enough, or didn’t love her enough, or wasn’t careful enough with what she meant to me.

That’s why I came in, really. It didn’t feel like normal creative doubt anymore. I can handle uncertainty in my work usually, but this felt heavier and harder to shake off. I couldn’t just brush it aside and say, “It’s fine, keep going.” It kept sticking.

It wasn’t like I thought someone else was putting them in my head—it was more like my own mind suddenly turned on this memorial piece and started attaching these terrible meanings to every choice I made.\n\nLike I’d be looking at a photo of my aunt and thinking, “Should this go here, or would the balance be better over there?” which is normal for me as an artist, but then this other thought would just slam in: “If you put it there, you’re dishonoring her,” or, “If you choose the wrong color, it means you didn’t love her enough.” I knew that didn’t make sense in a rational way, but it still felt like it came from inside my own head, and I couldn’t brush it off like I usually can with ordinary creative doubts.\n\nDoctor: Have these unwanted thoughts about the memorial piece been happening only when you're working on it, or have they been popping up at other times as well?\n\nPatient: Mostly when I’m actually working on it, or even when I’m about to work on it and I’m thinking through the choices. Like if I’m sitting there with the photos laid out, or I’m looking at colors, that’s when it really grabs onto everything.\n\nIt’s not just a normal, “Oh, that composition isn’t quite right,” which I deal with all the time in my work. It becomes this awful thought that if I put one picture in the wrong spot, or choose the wrong shade, then somehow I’m dishonoring my aunt or it means I didn’t love her enough. And I know that sounds extreme, because logically I know a color choice doesn’t prove how I felt about her, but in the moment it feels so loaded that I can’t just move on from it.\n\nSo I’d say it’s tied to the memorial piece specifically—not just random thoughts out of nowhere all day—but it can start even before I’m physically working on it, if I’m thinking about what I still need to decide.\n\nDoctor: Have these thoughts been affecting your daily life or relationships in any significant way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’m still functioning, mostly. I don’t want to make it sound like everything has completely fallen apart, because I am still getting most of my work done, and I can still show up for things in general.\n\nBut it’s definitely costing me time in the studio. What should be a normal decision—like arranging something or choosing a color—can turn into this long, exhausting process where I’m stuck and rechecking myself or just sitting there trying to feel okay about it. And that extra time has started to have real consequences.\n\nI missed a grant deadline because I lost so much time going back and forth on the memorial piece. That felt awful, because it wasn’t like I didn’t care about the grant—I did. I just couldn’t get myself unstuck enough to finish what I needed to submit. And I also backed out of a gallery talk that I had actually wanted to do, which is embarrassing to admit. I was already drained and behind, and I just felt like I couldn’t handle it on top of everything.\n\nSo relationships… I don’t know if it’s causing major conflict with people, exactly, but my daily work life has definitely been affected more than I want to admit.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having these unwanted thoughts about the memorial piece continuously since they first started, or have they come and gone?\n\nPatient: They’ve kind of come and gone, but… not in a clean way, if that makes sense. It’s not like I had one thought once and then it disappeared. It kept coming back around the memorial piece, especially whenever I was making decisions about it.\n\nLike, I’d be placing a photo or thinking about a color, and suddenly the thought would hit me that if I chose wrong, I was somehow dishonoring my aunt, or that it meant I didn’t love her enough. And then sometimes I could step away and it would quiet down for a bit, but the next time I came back to the piece, there it was again. It felt repetitive and unwanted, not like normal artistic second-guessing. I mean, I second-guess my work all the time, but this had this horrible emotional weight to it that I couldn’t just brush off.\n\nDoctor: How much distress would you say these unwanted thoughts have been causing you?\n\nPatient: It’s been pretty distressing, honestly. Not constant panic or anything, but when it hits, it feels awful and really disproportionate to what I’m actually doing.\n\nI came in because when I’m working on my aunt’s memorial piece, I keep getting these thoughts that feel… almost cruel, like if I put one photo in the wrong place or pick the wrong color, then I’m somehow dishonoring her. Or that it means I didn’t love her enough, which is such a painful thing to have pop into my head when the whole reason I’m making the piece is because I loved her.\n\nAnd I know, rationally, that a color choice or a layout decision doesn’t prove that. I can say that out loud and believe it on some level. But in the moment, it doesn’t feel like ordinary creative doubt. It feels like this heavy, guilty, urgent thing that I can’t just brush off. So I’d say the distress is definitely there—maybe not completely disabling, but enough that it really gets under my skin and makes working on it feel emotionally exhausting.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain aspects of the memorial piece or similar projects because of these unwanted thoughts?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, because I don’t think I’ve been avoiding it in a clear, deliberate way, like, “I refuse to work on this part.” It’s more slippery than that.\n\nI do sometimes find myself kind of circling around the harder decisions, if that counts. Like I’ll tell myself I’m just gathering materials, or I’m just cleaning up the studio, or I’m just looking at references before I commit to something. And sometimes that’s normal for my process, honestly, so it’s hard for me to separate what’s avoidance from what’s just how I work.\n\nWith similar projects… I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed a pattern as clearly there. The memorial piece feels so specific and emotionally loaded that I’m not sure I can say it generalizes to everything else. I guess I’d say I’m not consciously avoiding a whole category of work, but I may be putting off certain decisions on this piece more than I’d like to admit.\n\nDoctor: Do these unwanted thoughts about the memorial piece ever pop into your mind when you're not actively thinking about the piece or working on it?\n\nPatient: Yes… sometimes. It’s definitely strongest when I’m actually working on the memorial piece, like when I’m choosing where a photo goes or trying to settle on a color, but it doesn’t stay neatly contained there.\n\nI’ll be doing something else and it’ll sort of pop back in, like, “What if that choice was wrong?” or “What if I’m making something that doesn’t honor her the way it should?” And then it turns into this awful thought that maybe a wrong decision means I didn’t love her enough, which feels so unfair, because the whole reason I’m making it is because I did love her and I want it to mean something.\n\nIt’s not like I’m sitting there intentionally thinking through the artwork. It just intrudes, and then I feel pulled back into it emotionally. I can tell myself, “This is just a photo placement, this is just a color choice,” but it doesn’t always land that way in the moment. It feels much heavier than normal creative doubt.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts affecting any relationships outside of your work or daily activities?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s been damaging relationships in a big, obvious way, like I’m not fighting with people over it or anything. But it has started affecting what I can show up for, and I guess that indirectly affects people.\n\nI’m still getting most of my actual work done, which is partly why I keep telling myself it’s not that serious. But the amount of extra time I’m losing in the studio—going back over decisions, hesitating, not moving forward as cleanly as I normally would—it’s started to have consequences. I missed a grant deadline because I just couldn’t get everything wrapped up in time, and that felt awful because it was something I really should have been able to submit for.\n\nAnd I also backed out of a gallery talk I had actually wanted to do. I told myself I was just too overwhelmed and that it wasn’t the right time, but honestly, the studio delays and the emotional weight of the piece were a big part of it. So relationships, maybe not directly, but opportunities and commitments, yes. It’s starting to spill over more than I’d like to admit.\n\nDoctor: When these unwanted thoughts come up, do you feel like they align with your own values and beliefs, or do they feel like they're in conflict with them?\n\nPatient: They feel very much in conflict with me. That’s part of why they scare me so much, I think.\n\nI mean, I loved my aunt. I still love her. The whole reason I wanted to make the memorial piece was to honor her, not to reduce her to some perfect arrangement of photos or the exact right shade of blue or whatever. So when the thought comes in like, “If you put that image there, maybe you’re dishonoring her,” or, “If this color feels wrong, maybe it means you didn’t care enough,” it feels… almost offensive? Like it’s attacking the thing I care about most.\n\nIt doesn’t feel like my actual belief. I don’t honestly believe love works that way. If someone else were making something for someone they loved, I would never think one design choice proved anything about their love. I would probably tell them that the intention matters, that grief is messy, that a memorial doesn’t have to be perfect to be meaningful.\n\nBut when it’s me, with her, those thoughts land differently. They feel intrusive and unfair, and I can’t always just brush them off like normal creative doubt. Normal doubt is more like, “Is this composition working?” This feels like, “What if this choice means something terrible about you?” And that really doesn’t match what I believe, but it still gets under my skin.\n\nDoctor: When these thoughts come to you, do you feel like they are a part of yourself or do they feel foreign and not aligned with who you are?\n\nPatient: They feel foreign. I mean, they happen in my mind, obviously, so in that sense they’re “mine,” but they don’t feel like they come from the part of me that actually knows my aunt or loves her.\n\nIt’s more like this harsh, unwanted voice cutting in while I’m trying to make something meaningful. I’ll be looking at a photo placement or a color choice, something that should just be part of the creative process, and suddenly it becomes, “What if this dishonors her?” or, “What if getting this wrong means you didn’t love her enough?” And that feels completely out of line with who I am.\n\nI don’t believe love is measured by whether a memorial piece is perfectly arranged. I came in because I couldn’t just brush those thoughts off like normal creative doubts. They felt intrusive, like they were attacking the whole purpose of the piece, not expressing what I really think or feel.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding working on the memorial piece or similar projects altogether because of these unwanted thoughts?\n\nPatient: Not altogether, no. I’m not completely avoiding it in the sense of refusing to go into the studio or abandoning the piece. I’m still working, and honestly I’m still getting most of my work done overall.\n\nBut I do think I avoid parts of it, or I slow down so much that it almost functions like avoidance. Like I’ll sit there with the memorial piece and spend way too long rechecking a choice or circling around a decision instead of actually moving forward. And then the day gets eaten up. So from the outside it might look like I’m working, but I’m losing a lot of time.\n\nAnd it has had real consequences already. I missed a grant deadline because I couldn’t get everything wrapped up in time, which was really upsetting because it was something I should have been able to submit. And I backed out of a gallery talk I had wanted to do. I told myself I was just overwhelmed, but the delays in the studio were a big part of it.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I’m avoiding all of it completely, but it’s definitely interfering more than I want to admit.\n\nDoctor: When these unwanted thoughts come up, do you feel an urge to do something to make them go away or to prevent the bad things you're thinking about from happening?\n\nPatient: Yes. I do, and that’s the part that makes it feel less like normal worrying and more like I’m getting pulled into something.\n\nWhen the thought hits, I’ll feel this pressure to fix it somehow, like I can’t just leave the layout alone. I’ll reread the label text again and again, checking if the wording sounds respectful enough or if some phrase could be taken the wrong way. And then I’ll nudge the same objects on the table, just a tiny bit, like moving a photo half an inch or adjusting the spacing, and I’ll tell myself I’m just refining it, but really I’m trying to get this uncomfortable feeling to stop.\n\nI also keep checking the layout photos over and over. I’ll take a picture, look at it, zoom in, compare it to the previous version, then maybe move something back to where it already was. It’s embarrassing because part of me knows it’s already fine. Like, if I stepped back, I could probably say, “This is good enough, this is thoughtful, this is done.” But it doesn’t feel settled, and I keep chasing that feeling of, okay, now it’s safe, now it’s right, now nothing bad is implied by it.\n\nAnd I don’t literally think moving one object will prevent something terrible in a logical way, but emotionally it feels like if I don’t check or adjust it, I’m leaving open the possibility that I’ve done something wrong. So I lose studio time trying to make the feeling go away.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that these unwanted thoughts are significantly interfering with your ability to enjoy your daily activities or causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. I mean, I’m still functioning, so part of me keeps wanting to say, “It’s not that bad,” because I am still getting most of my work done. I’m not just lying in bed all day or totally unable to make things.\n\nBut it is interfering. The amount of extra time I lose in the studio is... it’s more than just being careful or perfectionistic. I’ll think I’m going in for a normal work session, and then somehow I’ve spent this huge chunk of time circling around the same decision instead of actually finishing anything. And that starts to drain the enjoyment out of it, because the work is supposed to feel meaningful, especially this piece, and instead it becomes tense and exhausting.\n\nAnd there have already been consequences. I missed a grant deadline because I couldn’t get everything finished and submitted in time, which felt awful because I really should have been able to do it. And I backed out of a gallery talk that I had actually wanted to do. It wasn’t that I didn’t care about it — I did — but I felt so behind and tangled up in the studio that I couldn’t face it.\n\nSo, yes, it’s causing distress. Maybe not ruining every part of my life, but it’s definitely affecting my work and making things I normally care about harder to enjoy.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the memorial piece needs to be perfectly symmetrical or exact in some way to feel right to you?\n\nPatient: Not exactly symmetrical, no. It’s not like I need everything measured with a ruler or perfectly mirrored. It’s more that it has to feel… settled, I guess. Like the arrangement has to land in a way where I don’t get that jolt of, “Wait, is this wrong?”\n\nSo I’ll end up rereading the label text again, and then I’ll look at the objects on the table and nudge the same ones over and over — just tiny adjustments, sometimes so small they probably don’t even matter. Then I’ll check the layout photos, compare them, zoom in, go back to the table, move something, take another photo, and check again.\n\nAnd the frustrating thing is that part of me knows it’s already fine. I can see that it’s thoughtful and that nothing is obviously wrong with it. But it doesn’t feel done until I get this internal sense of, okay, that’s settled. And I keep chasing that feeling, which means I lose a lot of studio time to it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you need to have complete control over every aspect of the memorial piece to feel comfortable with it?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say complete control over every aspect, exactly. That sounds almost too broad, like I’m trying to control the whole meaning of the piece or how every person will react to it, and I know I can’t really do that.\n\nBut with the actual layout, yes, I get pulled into needing it to feel controlled enough that I can relax. Like I’ll reread the label text again, even if I already read it five minutes ago, because I’m checking whether it lands the right way. Then I’ll look at the table and nudge the same objects, sometimes barely moving them, just trying to get the arrangement to feel settled. And then I’ll take or check the layout photos over and over, zooming in and comparing them, as if one more look will tell me, “Okay, it’s fine now.”\n\nThe strange thing is that part of me does know it’s already fine. It’s not like I always find an actual problem. A lot of the time I’m just stuck trying to make the discomfort go away. I keep thinking, if I can just get this one thing placed right, or this wording checked one more time, then I’ll feel comfortable with it. But then that feeling doesn’t last, or it doesn’t come at all, and I lose studio time going around in circles.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the memorial piece has to be perfect in order for you to feel like you've done your aunt justice?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s pretty much it, though I know “perfect” sounds dramatic. It’s not like I honestly believe there is one flawless version of the piece and anything else is worthless. But emotionally, when I’m in it, it starts to feel that way.\n\nI keep having these thoughts that if I choose the wrong photo, or place something slightly wrong, or use a color that doesn’t feel right, then somehow I’ve dishonored her. Like it would mean I didn’t understand her, or didn’t love her enough, or wasn’t careful enough with her memory. And I know, saying it out loud, that one photo placement doesn’t actually prove how much I loved my aunt. I do know that. But the thought hits me as if it’s true, and it’s really hard to just let it pass.\n\nThat’s actually why I came in. It stopped feeling like normal creative uncertainty. I’m used to having doubts in the studio — that’s part of making work — but this feels more awful and sticky. It’s unwanted, and it comes with this feeling that I could do something wrong that would be disrespectful to her, even if no one else would see it that way. So, yes, in that sense, it feels like it has to be exactly right before I can feel like I’ve done right by her.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your aunt's memory would be tarnished if the memorial piece isn't perfect?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s the fear, I think. “Tarnished” maybe sounds a little intense, but that’s kind of how it feels in the moment. Like if the piece isn’t right — if I choose the wrong photo, or the color feels off, or something is placed in a way that doesn’t feel careful enough — then it’s not just an art decision anymore. It starts to feel like I’ve done something disrespectful to her memory.\n\nAnd I know that doesn’t fully make sense. I mean, if someone else said that to me, I’d probably tell them, of course one photo placement doesn’t mean you didn’t love your aunt. But when I was working on it, I kept getting these awful, unwanted thoughts that I was going to dishonor her somehow. Like the piece would reveal that I didn’t understand her well enough, or didn’t love her enough, or wasn’t careful enough with what she meant to me.\n\nThat’s why I came in, really. It didn’t feel like normal creative doubt anymore. I can handle uncertainty in my work usually, but this felt heavier and harder to shake off. I couldn’t just brush it aside and say, “It’s fine, keep going.” It kept sticking.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. So, yes, in that sense, it feels like it has to be exactly right before I can feel like I’ve done right by her.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your aunt's memory would be tarnished if the memorial piece isn't perfect?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s the fear, I think. “Tarnished” maybe sounds a little intense, but that’s kind of how it feels in the moment. Like if the piece isn’t right — if I choose the wrong photo, or the color feels off, or something is placed in a way that doesn’t feel careful enough — then it’s not just an art decision anymore. It starts to feel like I’ve done something disrespectful to her memory.\n\nAnd I know that doesn’t fully make sense. I mean, if someone else said that to me, I’d probably tell them, of course one photo placement doesn’t mean you didn’t love your aunt. But when I was working on it, I kept getting these awful, unwanted thoughts that I was going to dishonor her somehow. Like the piece would reveal that I didn’t understand her well enough, or didn’t love her enough, or wasn’t careful enough with what she meant to me.\n\nThat’s why I came in, really. It didn’t feel like normal creative doubt anymore. I can handle uncertainty in my work usually, but this felt heavier and harder to shake off. I couldn’t just brush it aside and say, “It’s fine, keep going.” It kept sticking.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like the memorial piece needs to be exactly as you envisioned it, without any deviations, to feel right to you?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 32 / artist_or_related_worker / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Obsessions\nmanifestation: \"I came in because when I was working on my aunt’s memorial piece, I kept getting these awful, unwanted thoughts that I was going to dishonor her somehow—like one wrong photo placement or color choice would mean I didn’t love her enough—and I couldn’t just brush them off like normal creative doubts.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not exactly as I envisioned it in the sense of, like, matching some original sketch perfectly. My vision for artwork changes all the time, and usually I’m okay with that. Sometimes deviations are even the best part.

But with this piece, the deviations started to feel loaded in a way they normally don’t. If a photo wasn’t placed where I thought it should be, or if a color shifted and suddenly didn’t feel right, my mind would jump to this really upsetting place — like, “What if this means I’m not honoring her properly?” or “What if this means I didn’t love her enough to get it right?” It sounds so harsh when I say it out loud, because I don’t actually believe love comes down to one image or one shade of fabric or whatever. But in the moment it felt really convincing.

That’s why I came in, honestly. I’m used to having creative doubts, and I can usually work through them. This was different. These thoughts felt awful and unwanted, and I couldn’t just brush them off. It wasn’t only, “Does the piece look good?” It became, “Am I dishonoring her somehow if this isn’t right?” And that made every small change feel much bigger than it should have.

Not constant panic or anything, but when it hits, it feels awful and really disproportionate to what I’m actually doing.\n\nI came in because when I’m working on my aunt’s memorial piece, I keep getting these thoughts that feel… almost cruel, like if I put one photo in the wrong place or pick the wrong color, then I’m somehow dishonoring her. Or that it means I didn’t love her enough, which is such a painful thing to have pop into my head when the whole reason I’m making the piece is because I loved her.\n\nAnd I know, rationally, that a color choice or a layout decision doesn’t prove that. I can say that out loud and believe it on some level. But in the moment, it doesn’t feel like ordinary creative doubt. It feels like this heavy, guilty, urgent thing that I can’t just brush off. I do, and that’s the part that makes it feel less like normal worrying and more like I’m getting pulled into something.\n\nWhen the thought hits, I’ll feel this pressure to fix it somehow, like I can’t just leave the layout alone. I’ll reread the label text again and again, checking if the wording sounds respectful enough or if some phrase could be taken the wrong way. I’ll take a picture, look at it, zoom in, compare it to the previous version, then maybe move something back to where it already was. It’s embarrassing because part of me knows it’s already fine. Like, if I stepped back, I could probably say, “This is good enough, this is thoughtful, this is done.” But it doesn’t feel settled, and I keep chasing that feeling of, okay, now it’s safe, now it’s right, now nothing bad is implied by it.\n\nAnd I don’t literally think moving one object will prevent something terrible in a logical way, but emotionally it feels like if I don’t check or adjust it, I’m leaving open the possibility that I’ve done something wrong. It’s not like I need everything measured with a ruler or perfectly mirrored. It’s more that it has to feel… settled, I guess. Like the arrangement has to land in a way where I don’t get that jolt of, “Wait, is this wrong?”\n\nSo I’ll end up rereading the label text again, and then I’ll look at the objects on the table and nudge the same ones over and over — just tiny adjustments, sometimes so small they probably don’t even matter. And then I’ll take or check the layout photos over and over, zooming in and comparing them, as if one more look will tell me, “Okay, it’s fine now.”\n\nThe strange thing is that part of me does know it’s already fine. I keep thinking, if I can just get this one thing placed right, or this wording checked one more time, then I’ll feel comfortable with it. Like if I’m sitting there with the photos laid out, or I’m looking at colors, that’s when it really grabs onto everything.\n\nIt’s not just a normal, “Oh, that composition isn’t quite right,” which I deal with all the time in my work. It becomes this awful thought that if I put one picture in the wrong spot, or choose the wrong shade, then somehow I’m dishonoring my aunt or it means I didn’t love her enough. And I know that sounds extreme, because logically I know a color choice doesn’t prove how I felt about her, but in the moment it feels so loaded that I can’t just move on from it.\n\nSo I’d say it’s tied to the memorial piece specifically—not just random thoughts out of nowhere all day—but it can start even before I’m physically working on it, if I’m thinking about what I still need to decide.\n\nDoctor: Have these thoughts been affecting your daily life or relationships in any significant way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’m still functioning, mostly. I don’t want to make it sound like everything has completely fallen apart, because I am still getting most of my work done, and I can still show up for things in general.\n\nBut it’s definitely costing me time in the studio. What should be a normal decision—like arranging something or choosing a color—can turn into this long, exhausting process where I’m stuck and rechecking myself or just sitting there trying to feel okay about it. And that extra time has started to have real consequences.\n\nI missed a grant deadline because I lost so much time going back and forth on the memorial piece. That felt awful, because it wasn’t like I didn’t care about the grant—I did. I just couldn’t get myself unstuck enough to finish what I needed to submit. And I also backed out of a gallery talk that I had actually wanted to do, which is embarrassing to admit. I was already drained and behind, and I just felt like I couldn’t handle it on top of everything.\n\nSo relationships… I don’t know if it’s causing major conflict with people, exactly, but my daily work life has definitely been affected more than I want to admit.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having these unwanted thoughts about the memorial piece continuously since they first started, or have they come and gone?\n\nPatient: They’ve kind of come and gone, but… not in a clean way, if that makes sense. It’s not like I had one thought once and then it disappeared. It kept coming back around the memorial piece, especially whenever I was making decisions about it.\n\nLike, I’d be placing a photo or thinking about a color, and suddenly the thought would hit me that if I chose wrong, I was somehow dishonoring my aunt, or that it meant I didn’t love her enough. And then sometimes I could step away and it would quiet down for a bit, but the next time I came back to the piece, there it was again. It felt repetitive and unwanted, not like normal artistic second-guessing. I mean, I second-guess my work all the time, but this had this horrible emotional weight to it that I couldn’t just brush off.\n\nDoctor: How much distress would you say these unwanted thoughts have been causing you?\n\nPatient: It’s been pretty distressing, honestly. Not constant panic or anything, but when it hits, it feels awful and really disproportionate to what I’m actually doing.\n\nI came in because when I’m working on my aunt’s memorial piece, I keep getting these thoughts that feel… almost cruel, like if I put one photo in the wrong place or pick the wrong color, then I’m somehow dishonoring her. Or that it means I didn’t love her enough, which is such a painful thing to have pop into my head when the whole reason I’m making the piece is because I loved her.\n\nAnd I know, rationally, that a color choice or a layout decision doesn’t prove that. I can say that out loud and believe it on some level. But in the moment, it doesn’t feel like ordinary creative doubt. It feels like this heavy, guilty, urgent thing that I can’t just brush off. So I’d say the distress is definitely there—maybe not completely disabling, but enough that it really gets under my skin and makes working on it feel emotionally exhausting.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain aspects of the memorial piece or similar projects because of these unwanted thoughts?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, because I don’t think I’ve been avoiding it in a clear, deliberate way, like, “I refuse to work on this part.” It’s more slippery than that.\n\nI do sometimes find myself kind of circling around the harder decisions, if that counts. Like I’ll tell myself I’m just gathering materials, or I’m just cleaning up the studio, or I’m just looking at references before I commit to something. And sometimes that’s normal for my process, honestly, so it’s hard for me to separate what’s avoidance from what’s just how I work.\n\nWith similar projects… I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed a pattern as clearly there. The memorial piece feels so specific and emotionally loaded that I’m not sure I can say it generalizes to everything else. I guess I’d say I’m not consciously avoiding a whole category of work, but I may be putting off certain decisions on this piece more than I’d like to admit.\n\nDoctor: Do these unwanted thoughts about the memorial piece ever pop into your mind when you're not actively thinking about the piece or working on it?\n\nPatient: Yes… sometimes. It’s definitely strongest when I’m actually working on the memorial piece, like when I’m choosing where a photo goes or trying to settle on a color, but it doesn’t stay neatly contained there.\n\nI’ll be doing something else and it’ll sort of pop back in, like, “What if that choice was wrong?” or “What if I’m making something that doesn’t honor her the way it should?” And then it turns into this awful thought that maybe a wrong decision means I didn’t love her enough, which feels so unfair, because the whole reason I’m making it is because I did love her and I want it to mean something.\n\nIt’s not like I’m sitting there intentionally thinking through the artwork. It just intrudes, and then I feel pulled back into it emotionally. I can tell myself, “This is just a photo placement, this is just a color choice,” but it doesn’t always land that way in the moment. It feels much heavier than normal creative doubt.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts affecting any relationships outside of your work or daily activities?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s been damaging relationships in a big, obvious way, like I’m not fighting with people over it or anything. But it has started affecting what I can show up for, and I guess that indirectly affects people.\n\nI’m still getting most of my actual work done, which is partly why I keep telling myself it’s not that serious. But the amount of extra time I’m losing in the studio—going back over decisions, hesitating, not moving forward as cleanly as I normally would—it’s started to have consequences. I missed a grant deadline because I just couldn’t get everything wrapped up in time, and that felt awful because it was something I really should have been able to submit for.\n\nAnd I also backed out of a gallery talk I had actually wanted to do. I told myself I was just too overwhelmed and that it wasn’t the right time, but honestly, the studio delays and the emotional weight of the piece were a big part of it. So relationships, maybe not directly, but opportunities and commitments, yes. It’s starting to spill over more than I’d like to admit.\n\nDoctor: When these unwanted thoughts come up, do you feel like they align with your own values and beliefs, or do they feel like they're in conflict with them?\n\nPatient: They feel very much in conflict with me. That’s part of why they scare me so much, I think.\n\nI mean, I loved my aunt. I still love her. The whole reason I wanted to make the memorial piece was to honor her, not to reduce her to some perfect arrangement of photos or the exact right shade of blue or whatever. So when the thought comes in like, “If you put that image there, maybe you’re dishonoring her,” or, “If this color feels wrong, maybe it means you didn’t care enough,” it feels… almost offensive? Like it’s attacking the thing I care about most.\n\nIt doesn’t feel like my actual belief. I don’t honestly believe love works that way. If someone else were making something for someone they loved, I would never think one design choice proved anything about their love. I would probably tell them that the intention matters, that grief is messy, that a memorial doesn’t have to be perfect to be meaningful.\n\nBut when it’s me, with her, those thoughts land differently. They feel intrusive and unfair, and I can’t always just brush them off like normal creative doubt. Normal doubt is more like, “Is this composition working?” This feels like, “What if this choice means something terrible about you?” And that really doesn’t match what I believe, but it still gets under my skin.\n\nDoctor: When these thoughts come to you, do you feel like they are a part of yourself or do they feel foreign and not aligned with who you are?\n\nPatient: They feel foreign. I mean, they happen in my mind, obviously, so in that sense they’re “mine,” but they don’t feel like they come from the part of me that actually knows my aunt or loves her.\n\nIt’s more like this harsh, unwanted voice cutting in while I’m trying to make something meaningful. I’ll be looking at a photo placement or a color choice, something that should just be part of the creative process, and suddenly it becomes, “What if this dishonors her?” or, “What if getting this wrong means you didn’t love her enough?” And that feels completely out of line with who I am.\n\nI don’t believe love is measured by whether a memorial piece is perfectly arranged. I came in because I couldn’t just brush those thoughts off like normal creative doubts. They felt intrusive, like they were attacking the whole purpose of the piece, not expressing what I really think or feel.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding working on the memorial piece or similar projects altogether because of these unwanted thoughts?\n\nPatient: Not altogether, no. I’m not completely avoiding it in the sense of refusing to go into the studio or abandoning the piece. I’m still working, and honestly I’m still getting most of my work done overall.\n\nBut I do think I avoid parts of it, or I slow down so much that it almost functions like avoidance. Like I’ll sit there with the memorial piece and spend way too long rechecking a choice or circling around a decision instead of actually moving forward. And then the day gets eaten up. So from the outside it might look like I’m working, but I’m losing a lot of time.\n\nAnd it has had real consequences already. I missed a grant deadline because I couldn’t get everything wrapped up in time, which was really upsetting because it was something I should have been able to submit. And I backed out of a gallery talk I had wanted to do. I told myself I was just overwhelmed, but the delays in the studio were a big part of it.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I’m avoiding all of it completely, but it’s definitely interfering more than I want to admit.\n\nDoctor: When these unwanted thoughts come up, do you feel an urge to do something to make them go away or to prevent the bad things you're thinking about from happening?\n\nPatient: Yes. I do, and that’s the part that makes it feel less like normal worrying and more like I’m getting pulled into something.\n\nWhen the thought hits, I’ll feel this pressure to fix it somehow, like I can’t just leave the layout alone. I’ll reread the label text again and again, checking if the wording sounds respectful enough or if some phrase could be taken the wrong way. And then I’ll nudge the same objects on the table, just a tiny bit, like moving a photo half an inch or adjusting the spacing, and I’ll tell myself I’m just refining it, but really I’m trying to get this uncomfortable feeling to stop.\n\nI also keep checking the layout photos over and over. I’ll take a picture, look at it, zoom in, compare it to the previous version, then maybe move something back to where it already was. It’s embarrassing because part of me knows it’s already fine. Like, if I stepped back, I could probably say, “This is good enough, this is thoughtful, this is done.” But it doesn’t feel settled, and I keep chasing that feeling of, okay, now it’s safe, now it’s right, now nothing bad is implied by it.\n\nAnd I don’t literally think moving one object will prevent something terrible in a logical way, but emotionally it feels like if I don’t check or adjust it, I’m leaving open the possibility that I’ve done something wrong. So I lose studio time trying to make the feeling go away.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that these unwanted thoughts are significantly interfering with your ability to enjoy your daily activities or causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. I mean, I’m still functioning, so part of me keeps wanting to say, “It’s not that bad,” because I am still getting most of my work done. I’m not just lying in bed all day or totally unable to make things.\n\nBut it is interfering. The amount of extra time I lose in the studio is... it’s more than just being careful or perfectionistic. I’ll think I’m going in for a normal work session, and then somehow I’ve spent this huge chunk of time circling around the same decision instead of actually finishing anything. And that starts to drain the enjoyment out of it, because the work is supposed to feel meaningful, especially this piece, and instead it becomes tense and exhausting.\n\nAnd there have already been consequences. I missed a grant deadline because I couldn’t get everything finished and submitted in time, which felt awful because I really should have been able to do it. And I backed out of a gallery talk that I had actually wanted to do. It wasn’t that I didn’t care about it — I did — but I felt so behind and tangled up in the studio that I couldn’t face it.\n\nSo, yes, it’s causing distress. Maybe not ruining every part of my life, but it’s definitely affecting my work and making things I normally care about harder to enjoy.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the memorial piece needs to be perfectly symmetrical or exact in some way to feel right to you?\n\nPatient: Not exactly symmetrical, no. It’s not like I need everything measured with a ruler or perfectly mirrored. It’s more that it has to feel… settled, I guess. Like the arrangement has to land in a way where I don’t get that jolt of, “Wait, is this wrong?”\n\nSo I’ll end up rereading the label text again, and then I’ll look at the objects on the table and nudge the same ones over and over — just tiny adjustments, sometimes so small they probably don’t even matter. Then I’ll check the layout photos, compare them, zoom in, go back to the table, move something, take another photo, and check again.\n\nAnd the frustrating thing is that part of me knows it’s already fine. I can see that it’s thoughtful and that nothing is obviously wrong with it. But it doesn’t feel done until I get this internal sense of, okay, that’s settled. And I keep chasing that feeling, which means I lose a lot of studio time to it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you need to have complete control over every aspect of the memorial piece to feel comfortable with it?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say complete control over every aspect, exactly. That sounds almost too broad, like I’m trying to control the whole meaning of the piece or how every person will react to it, and I know I can’t really do that.\n\nBut with the actual layout, yes, I get pulled into needing it to feel controlled enough that I can relax. Like I’ll reread the label text again, even if I already read it five minutes ago, because I’m checking whether it lands the right way. Then I’ll look at the table and nudge the same objects, sometimes barely moving them, just trying to get the arrangement to feel settled. And then I’ll take or check the layout photos over and over, zooming in and comparing them, as if one more look will tell me, “Okay, it’s fine now.”\n\nThe strange thing is that part of me does know it’s already fine. It’s not like I always find an actual problem. A lot of the time I’m just stuck trying to make the discomfort go away. I keep thinking, if I can just get this one thing placed right, or this wording checked one more time, then I’ll feel comfortable with it. But then that feeling doesn’t last, or it doesn’t come at all, and I lose studio time going around in circles.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the memorial piece has to be perfect in order for you to feel like you've done your aunt justice?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s pretty much it, though I know “perfect” sounds dramatic. It’s not like I honestly believe there is one flawless version of the piece and anything else is worthless. But emotionally, when I’m in it, it starts to feel that way.\n\nI keep having these thoughts that if I choose the wrong photo, or place something slightly wrong, or use a color that doesn’t feel right, then somehow I’ve dishonored her. Like it would mean I didn’t understand her, or didn’t love her enough, or wasn’t careful enough with her memory. And I know, saying it out loud, that one photo placement doesn’t actually prove how much I loved my aunt. I do know that. But the thought hits me as if it’s true, and it’s really hard to just let it pass.\n\nThat’s actually why I came in. It stopped feeling like normal creative uncertainty. I’m used to having doubts in the studio — that’s part of making work — but this feels more awful and sticky. It’s unwanted, and it comes with this feeling that I could do something wrong that would be disrespectful to her, even if no one else would see it that way. So, yes, in that sense, it feels like it has to be exactly right before I can feel like I’ve done right by her.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your aunt's memory would be tarnished if the memorial piece isn't perfect?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s the fear, I think. “Tarnished” maybe sounds a little intense, but that’s kind of how it feels in the moment. Like if the piece isn’t right — if I choose the wrong photo, or the color feels off, or something is placed in a way that doesn’t feel careful enough — then it’s not just an art decision anymore. It starts to feel like I’ve done something disrespectful to her memory.\n\nAnd I know that doesn’t fully make sense. I mean, if someone else said that to me, I’d probably tell them, of course one photo placement doesn’t mean you didn’t love your aunt. But when I was working on it, I kept getting these awful, unwanted thoughts that I was going to dishonor her somehow. Like the piece would reveal that I didn’t understand her well enough, or didn’t love her enough, or wasn’t careful enough with what she meant to me.\n\nThat’s why I came in, really. It didn’t feel like normal creative doubt anymore. I can handle uncertainty in my work usually, but this felt heavier and harder to shake off. I couldn’t just brush it aside and say, “It’s fine, keep going.” It kept sticking.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the memorial piece needs to be exactly as you envisioned it, without any deviations, to feel right to you?\n\nPatient: Not exactly as I envisioned it in the sense of, like, matching some original sketch perfectly. My vision for artwork changes all the time, and usually I’m okay with that. Sometimes deviations are even the best part.\n\nBut with this piece, the deviations started to feel loaded in a way they normally don’t. If a photo wasn’t placed where I thought it should be, or if a color shifted and suddenly didn’t feel right, my mind would jump to this really upsetting place — like, “What if this means I’m not honoring her properly?” or “What if this means I didn’t love her enough to get it right?” It sounds so harsh when I say it out loud, because I don’t actually believe love comes down to one image or one shade of fabric or whatever. But in the moment it felt really convincing.\n\nThat’s why I came in, honestly. I’m used to having creative doubts, and I can usually work through them. This was different. These thoughts felt awful and unwanted, and I couldn’t just brush them off. It wasn’t only, “Does the piece look good?” It became, “Am I dishonoring her somehow if this isn’t right?” And that made every small change feel much bigger than it should have.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. That’s actually one of the main reasons I came in, because it wasn’t just that I was thinking about the piece a lot in a normal artist way. I mean, I’m used to carrying a project around in my head — thinking about composition while I’m making coffee, waking up with an idea, going back and forth on details. That part is familiar to me.

But this felt different. I would want to stop thinking about it, and I just… couldn’t really let it go. Especially the thoughts about whether I was somehow going to dishonor her. It would be something tiny, like one photo being placed slightly wrong, or a color not feeling exactly right, and then my mind would latch onto it like it meant something huge — like it meant I didn’t love her enough, or didn’t understand her well enough, or wasn’t being careful enough with her memory.

And I knew, at least logically, that that was not a reasonable conclusion. I know a color choice doesn’t equal how much I loved my aunt. But the thought would still come back and feel awful and urgent, and I couldn’t just brush it off the way I usually can with normal creative doubts. It kept circling back, even when I was tired of it and wanted to move on.

I’ll be looking at a photo placement or a color choice, something that should just be part of the creative process, and suddenly it becomes, “What if this dishonors her?” or, “What if getting this wrong means you didn’t love her enough?” And that feels completely out of line with who I am.\n\nI don’t believe love is measured by whether a memorial piece is perfectly arranged. I came in because I couldn’t just brush those thoughts off like normal creative doubts. Like, if I stepped back, I could probably say, “This is good enough, this is thoughtful, this is done.” But it doesn’t feel settled, and I keep chasing that feeling of, okay, now it’s safe, now it’s right, now nothing bad is implied by it.\n\nAnd I don’t literally think moving one object will prevent something terrible in a logical way, but emotionally it feels like if I don’t check or adjust it, I’m leaving open the possibility that I’ve done something wrong. And then I’ll take or check the layout photos over and over, zooming in and comparing them, as if one more look will tell me, “Okay, it’s fine now.”\n\nThe strange thing is that part of me does know it’s already fine. It’s not like I always find an actual problem. A lot of the time I’m just stuck trying to make the discomfort go away. I keep thinking, if I can just get this one thing placed right, or this wording checked one more time, then I’ll feel comfortable with it. But then that feeling doesn’t last, or it doesn’t come at all, and I lose studio time going around in circles.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the memorial piece has to be perfect in order for you to feel like you've done your aunt justice?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s pretty much it, though I know “perfect” sounds dramatic. It’s not like I honestly believe there is one flawless version of the piece and anything else is worthless. Like it would mean I didn’t understand her, or didn’t love her enough, or wasn’t careful enough with her memory. And I know, saying it out loud, that one photo placement doesn’t actually prove how much I loved my aunt. I do know that. But the thought hits me as if it’s true, and it’s really hard to just let it pass.\n\nThat’s actually why I came in. It stopped feeling like normal creative uncertainty. I’m used to having doubts in the studio — that’s part of making work — but this feels more awful and sticky. It’s unwanted, and it comes with this feeling that I could do something wrong that would be disrespectful to her, even if no one else would see it that way. So, yes, in that sense, it feels like it has to be exactly right before I can feel like I’ve done right by her.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your aunt's memory would be tarnished if the memorial piece isn't perfect?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s the fear, I think. “Tarnished” maybe sounds a little intense, but that’s kind of how it feels in the moment. Like if the piece isn’t right — if I choose the wrong photo, or the color feels off, or something is placed in a way that doesn’t feel careful enough — then it’s not just an art decision anymore. It starts to feel like I’ve done something disrespectful to her memory.\n\nAnd I know that doesn’t fully make sense. I mean, if someone else said that to me, I’d probably tell them, of course one photo placement doesn’t mean you didn’t love your aunt. But when I was working on it, I kept getting these awful, unwanted thoughts that I was going to dishonor her somehow. Like the piece would reveal that I didn’t understand her well enough, or didn’t love her enough, or wasn’t careful enough with what she meant to me.\n\nThat’s why I came in, really. It didn’t feel like normal creative doubt anymore. I can handle uncertainty in my work usually, but this felt heavier and harder to shake off. I couldn’t just brush it aside and say, “It’s fine, keep going.” It kept sticking.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the memorial piece needs to be exactly as you envisioned it, without any deviations, to feel right to you?\n\nPatient: Not exactly as I envisioned it in the sense of, like, matching some original sketch perfectly. My vision for artwork changes all the time, and usually I’m okay with that. Sometimes deviations are even the best part.\n\nBut with this piece, the deviations started to feel loaded in a way they normally don’t. If a photo wasn’t placed where I thought it should be, or if a color shifted and suddenly didn’t feel right, my mind would jump to this really upsetting place — like, “What if this means I’m not honoring her properly?” or “What if this means I didn’t love her enough to get it right?” It sounds so harsh when I say it out loud, because I don’t actually believe love comes down to one image or one shade of fabric or whatever. But in the moment it felt really convincing.\n\nThat’s why I came in, honestly. I’m used to having creative doubts, and I can usually work through them. This was different. These thoughts felt awful and unwanted, and I couldn’t just brush them off. It wasn’t only, “Does the piece look good?” It became, “Am I dishonoring her somehow if this isn’t right?” And that made every small change feel much bigger than it should have.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you cannot stop thinking about the memorial piece even when you want to?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually one of the main reasons I came in, because it wasn’t just that I was thinking about the piece a lot in a normal artist way. I mean, I’m used to carrying a project around in my head — thinking about composition while I’m making coffee, waking up with an idea, going back and forth on details. That part is familiar to me.\n\nBut this felt different. I would want to stop thinking about it, and I just… couldn’t really let it go. Especially the thoughts about whether I was somehow going to dishonor her. It would be something tiny, like one photo being placed slightly wrong, or a color not feeling exactly right, and then my mind would latch onto it like it meant something huge — like it meant I didn’t love her enough, or didn’t understand her well enough, or wasn’t being careful enough with her memory.\n\nAnd I knew, at least logically, that that was not a reasonable conclusion. I know a color choice doesn’t equal how much I loved my aunt. But the thought would still come back and feel awful and urgent, and I couldn’t just brush it off the way I usually can with normal creative doubts. It kept circling back, even when I was tired of it and wanted to move on."
